I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "question": "Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?"

I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.

Yes… I think that’s part of it. I just don’t have the push I used to. Like choir—I used to get myself dressed and go, and quilting too, and visiting people. Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.

I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.

My neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push like I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know—there was one lady from church, she always liked when I brought those little squares, the blue ones were her favorite—anyway, now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. That sounds like a big hollow room or something. Sometimes I’m just… not moving much, kind of sitting there, and then I’ll notice the clock ticking too loud. My mother had a clock like that in the kitchen, always ran a little fast.\n\nSo maybe it’s sad, maybe it’s just being tired in my head. I can’t really tell the difference sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you don’t have any energy, or that even small tasks take a lot out of you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I just don’t have the push I used to. Like choir—I used to get myself dressed and go, and quilting too, and visiting people. Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.\n\nMost days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Not even really watching it. And I know folks are expecting me sometimes, or maybe wondering where I am, but I just… don’t get up and go. The other day I thought about calling someone, and then I saw a thread on my sleeve and started picking at it instead. So yes, small things do take a lot out of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it’s just a little or not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.\n\nMy neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose… if I think about choir, I can remember liking it, the way the voices all came together. And quilting, I used to feel good seeing the little squares line up right.

But now it’s like there’s no push in me to get there, or even to start. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.

So I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.

Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.\n\nMost days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Not even really watching it. And I know folks are expecting me sometimes, or maybe wondering where I am, but I just… don’t get up and go. The other day I thought about calling someone, and then I saw a thread on my sleeve and started picking at it instead. So yes, small things do take a lot out of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it’s just a little or not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.\n\nMy neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir, quilting, or visiting people, do you still get any pleasure or satisfaction from them, even if you don’t end up doing them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I think about choir, I can remember liking it, the way the voices all came together. And quilting, I used to feel good seeing the little squares line up right.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no push in me to get there, or even to start. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.

I guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.

It’s more like I just don’t get moving. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir, quilting, or visiting folks, and most days I sit at home with the TV on low even though I know people are expecting me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. But now when I think of them, it’s more like I know I ought to want to go, but I don’t feel that push.

Most days I just end up at home, TV on low. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.

I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\nPatient: I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. But now when I think of them, it’s more like I know I ought to want to go, but I don’t feel that push.\n\nMost days I just end up at home, TV on low. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that most days, even if not all the time, you feel down, sad, or low in mood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not real sure. Some days I feel kind of… flat, I guess, but I don’t always know if that’s sad. My daughter asks me that too sometimes, and then I start thinking about whether I slept right or if I ate, and then I forget what I was answering.

So, most days? I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed it like that, like crying or saying “I’m sad” every day. It’s more just sitting there and the day goes by.

I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know—there was one lady from church, she always liked when I brought those little squares, the blue ones were her favorite—anyway, now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. My mother had a clock like that in the kitchen, always ran a little fast.\n\nSo maybe it’s sad, maybe it’s just being tired in my head. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it persistent sadness. That sounds more… steady, like a rain that doesn’t stop. Mine isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m just quiet, or I’m sitting and the TV’s talking more than I am.

Low, maybe some days. But most days? I get mixed up trying to say. Yesterday I meant to fold towels and then I found an old church bulletin in the drawer, and I was thinking about who used to sit in the third pew… so I don’t always keep track of my mood like that. I can’t say for sure.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push like I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know—there was one lady from church, she always liked when I brought those little squares, the blue ones were her favorite—anyway, now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. That sounds like a big hollow room or something. Sometimes I’m just… not moving much, kind of sitting there, and then I’ll notice the clock ticking too loud. My mother had a clock like that in the kitchen, always ran a little fast.\n\nSo maybe it’s sad, maybe it’s just being tired in my head. I can’t really tell the difference sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you don’t have any energy, or that even small tasks take a lot out of you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I just don’t have the push I used to. Like choir—I used to get myself dressed and go, and quilting too, and visiting people. Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.\n\nMost days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Not even really watching it. And I know folks are expecting me sometimes, or maybe wondering where I am, but I just… don’t get up and go. The other day I thought about calling someone, and then I saw a thread on my sleeve and started picking at it instead. So yes, small things do take a lot out of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it’s just a little or not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.\n\nMy neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir, quilting, or visiting people, do you still get any pleasure or satisfaction from them, even if you don’t end up doing them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I think about choir, I can remember liking it, the way the voices all came together. And quilting, I used to feel good seeing the little squares line up right.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no push in me to get there, or even to start. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\nPatient: I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. But now when I think of them, it’s more like I know I ought to want to go, but I don’t feel that push.\n\nMost days I just end up at home, TV on low. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, even if not all the time, you feel down, sad, or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. Some days I feel kind of… flat, I guess, but I don’t always know if that’s sad. I haven’t noticed it like that, like crying or saying “I’m sad” every day. It’s more just sitting there and the day goes by.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a persistent sadness or low mood, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it persistent sadness. I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My daughter asks me that too sometimes, and then I start thinking about whether I slept right or if I ate, and then I forget what I was answering.\n\nSo, most days? I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed it like that, like crying or saying “I’m sad” every day. It’s more just sitting there and the day goes by.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a persistent sadness or low mood, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it persistent sadness. That sounds more… steady, like a rain that doesn’t stop. Mine isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m just quiet, or I’m sitting and the TV’s talking more than I am.\n\nLow, maybe some days. But most days? I get mixed up trying to say. Yesterday I meant to fold towels and then I found an old church bulletin in the drawer, and I was thinking about who used to sit in the third pew… so I don’t always keep track of my mood like that. I can’t say for sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that, on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I’m trying to think how to say it right. I don’t wake up and say, “I’m sad,” not most days like that. Sometimes I’m just sort of there, you know, and the house is quiet except the television.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push like I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know—there was one lady from church, she always liked when I brought those little squares, the blue ones were her favorite—anyway, now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. That sounds like a big hollow room or something. Sometimes I’m just… not moving much, kind of sitting there, and then I’ll notice the clock ticking too loud. My mother had a clock like that in the kitchen, always ran a little fast.\n\nSo maybe it’s sad, maybe it’s just being tired in my head. I can’t really tell the difference sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you don’t have any energy, or that even small tasks take a lot out of you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I just don’t have the push I used to. Like choir—I used to get myself dressed and go, and quilting too, and visiting people. Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.\n\nMost days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Not even really watching it. And I know folks are expecting me sometimes, or maybe wondering where I am, but I just… don’t get up and go. The other day I thought about calling someone, and then I saw a thread on my sleeve and started picking at it instead. So yes, small things do take a lot out of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it’s just a little or not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.\n\nMy neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir, quilting, or visiting people, do you still get any pleasure or satisfaction from them, even if you don’t end up doing them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I think about choir, I can remember liking it, the way the voices all came together. And quilting, I used to feel good seeing the little squares line up right.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no push in me to get there, or even to start. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\nPatient: I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, even if not all the time, you feel down, sad, or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. Some days I feel kind of… flat, I guess, but I don’t always know if that’s sad. I know you asked me that already, and I’m trying not to go in circles. I just… I don’t really have a clear answer. Some days feel kind of dull, like the morning just turns into afternoon, but I don’t know if that means sad.

I used to know the days by when the mailman came, because he’d come right after that game show—well, the old one, not the new host—and now I don’t pay attention the same. So, most days low? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it that plain.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push like I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know—there was one lady from church, she always liked when I brought those little squares, the blue ones were her favorite—anyway, now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. That sounds like a big hollow room or something. Sometimes I’m just… not moving much, kind of sitting there, and then I’ll notice the clock ticking too loud. My mother had a clock like that in the kitchen, always ran a little fast.\n\nSo maybe it’s sad, maybe it’s just being tired in my head. I can’t really tell the difference sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you don’t have any energy, or that even small tasks take a lot out of you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I just don’t have the push I used to. Like choir—I used to get myself dressed and go, and quilting too, and visiting people. Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.\n\nMost days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Not even really watching it. And I know folks are expecting me sometimes, or maybe wondering where I am, but I just… don’t get up and go. The other day I thought about calling someone, and then I saw a thread on my sleeve and started picking at it instead. So yes, small things do take a lot out of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it’s just a little or not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.\n\nMy neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir, quilting, or visiting people, do you still get any pleasure or satisfaction from them, even if you don’t end up doing them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I think about choir, I can remember liking it, the way the voices all came together. And quilting, I used to feel good seeing the little squares line up right.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no push in me to get there, or even to start. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\nPatient: I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. But now when I think of them, it’s more like I know I ought to want to go, but I don’t feel that push.\n\nMost days I just end up at home, TV on low. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, even if not all the time, you feel down, sad, or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. Some days I feel kind of… flat, I guess, but I don’t always know if that’s sad. My daughter asks me that too sometimes, and then I start thinking about whether I slept right or if I ate, and then I forget what I was answering.\n\nSo, most days? I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed it like that, like crying or saying “I’m sad” every day. It’s more just sitting there and the day goes by.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a persistent sadness or low mood, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it persistent sadness. That sounds more… steady, like a rain that doesn’t stop. Mine isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m just quiet, or I’m sitting and the TV’s talking more than I am.\n\nLow, maybe some days. But most days? I get mixed up trying to say. Yesterday I meant to fold towels and then I found an old church bulletin in the drawer, and I was thinking about who used to sit in the third pew… so I don’t always keep track of my mood like that. I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to say it right. I don’t wake up and say, “I’m sad,” not most days like that. Sometimes I’m just sort of there, you know, and the house is quiet except the television.\n\nBut then I’ll start thinking about whether I fed the cat—well, I don’t have a cat now, but we had one years ago—and then my mind goes off. So I don’t know if that counts as low mood. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I know you asked me that already, and I’m trying not to go in circles. I just… I don’t really have a clear answer. Some days feel kind of dull, like the morning just turns into afternoon, but I don’t know if that means sad.\n\nI used to know the days by when the mailman came, because he’d come right after that game show—well, the old one, not the new host—and now I don’t pay attention the same. So, most days low? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it that plain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\nPatient: I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. But now when I think of them, it’s more like I know I ought to want to go, but I don’t feel that push.\n\nMost days I just end up at home, TV on low. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, even if not all the time, you feel down, sad, or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. Some days I feel kind of… flat, I guess, but I don’t always know if that’s sad. My daughter asks me that too sometimes, and then I start thinking about whether I slept right or if I ate, and then I forget what I was answering.\n\nSo, most days? I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed it like that, like crying or saying “I’m sad” every day. It’s more just sitting there and the day goes by.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a persistent sadness or low mood, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it persistent sadness. That sounds more… steady, like a rain that doesn’t stop. Mine isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m just quiet, or I’m sitting and the TV’s talking more than I am.\n\nLow, maybe some days. But most days? I get mixed up trying to say. Yesterday I meant to fold towels and then I found an old church bulletin in the drawer, and I was thinking about who used to sit in the third pew… so I don’t always keep track of my mood like that. I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to say it right. I don’t wake up and say, “I’m sad,” not most days like that. Sometimes I’m just sort of there, you know, and the house is quiet except the television.\n\nBut then I’ll start thinking about whether I fed the cat—well, I don’t have a cat now, but we had one years ago—and then my mind goes off. So I don’t know if that counts as low mood. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I know you asked me that already, and I’m trying not to go in circles. I just… I don’t really have a clear answer. Some days feel kind of dull, like the morning just turns into afternoon, but I don’t know if that means sad.\n\nI used to know the days by when the mailman came, because he’d come right after that game show—well, the old one, not the new host—and now I don’t pay attention the same. So, most days low? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it that plain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know people are expecting me sometimes, and I just… don’t get myself up to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without much motivation or interest in doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, I’d say it started after I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. I used to keep track of all the little papers and attendance slips—funny how busy that kept me—but since then it’s just kind of been building, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or hopeless most days, or feeling like you're not worth much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless, exactly. I mean, some mornings are heavier than others, and I look around at the dishes or the sewing basket and think, “Well, that’s just sitting there again.” But then I get thinking about how my mother used to keep all her thread in old cookie tins—those blue Danish ones, you know—and I still have one somewhere.\n\nAs far as not being worth much… I don’t really sit and say that to myself, no. It’s more like I just don’t get moving. I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or appetite lately, or do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Sleep and eating… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept track. Some nights I’m up looking at the ceiling, but then I remember I forgot to put the porch light on, and then I get thinking about when my husband used to say that light brought every moth in the county.\n\nAppetite, well… I eat what’s there, I suppose. Nothing special to say. And guilty… I’m not sure. I don’t sit around naming it that. Maybe I feel bad when I miss something I meant to do, but guilty sounds like a bigger word than what I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or sad most days, even if it's not necessarily hopeless or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Sad most days… I don’t know if I’d say it like that. Some days I’m just sitting there and the house feels too quiet, but then I’ll notice the curtains need washing, or I’ll think about that little grocery on Maple that used to have the good peaches—oh, they were so much better than what they sell now.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep count of “most days.” It’s more like I get slowed down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer “most days.” I don’t sit there thinking, “I’m sad now,” you know? Some days I feel kind of low or slow, but then I get looking at the dust on the windowsill, and I remember my aunt used to say if you could write your name in it, it was time to clean.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes. I wouldn’t know if it’s most days. It’s not something I’ve been counting.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, when you’re feeling slowed down or low, it’s often accompanied by a feeling of sadness or emptiness, even if it doesn’t feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. That sounds like a big hollow room or something. Sometimes I’m just… not moving much, kind of sitting there, and then I’ll notice the clock ticking too loud. My mother had a clock like that in the kitchen, always ran a little fast.\n\nSo maybe it’s sad, maybe it’s just being tired in my head. I can’t really tell the difference sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you don’t have any energy, or that even small tasks take a lot out of you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I just don’t have the push I used to. Like choir—I used to get myself dressed and go, and quilting too, and visiting people. Now even thinking about getting ready feels like a lot.\n\nMost days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Not even really watching it. And I know folks are expecting me sometimes, or maybe wondering where I am, but I just… don’t get up and go. The other day I thought about calling someone, and then I saw a thread on my sleeve and started picking at it instead. So yes, small things do take a lot out of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel down or sad, even if it’s just a little or not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most days. It’s more like… I get quiet and slow, and then I forget what I was going to do. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, but I don’t know if that’s sadness or just being worn down.\n\nMy neighbor used to say you can tell a person’s mood by whether their curtains are open, but mine are half-open a lot because the rod sticks on one side. So… I don’t know. Maybe a little sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir, quilting, or visiting people, do you still get any pleasure or satisfaction from them, even if you don’t end up doing them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I think about choir, I can remember liking it, the way the voices all came together. And quilting, I used to feel good seeing the little squares line up right.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no push in me to get there, or even to start. Visiting folks too—I know people might be expecting me, and I used to enjoy that, sitting and talking a while. These days I mostly just stay home with the TV on low, and sometimes it’s not even a program I care about. It’s just on.\n\nSo I don’t know if I get much pleasure from thinking about those things now. Mostly I just think, “I should go,” and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, your mood has been down or low more days than not in the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: More days than not… I don’t know how to count it, really. Some mornings I’m just slow getting up, but I wouldn’t always call it low. It’s like when the kettle sits there and you forget you put the water on—well, mine’s electric now, my daughter said it’s safer.\n\nI guess there are days I feel kind of heavy, but other days I’m just sitting there, not much of anything. So I’m not sure I can say it’s been down more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like choir or quilting, do you still feel any joy or interest in them, even if you aren't doing them now?\n\nPatient: I used to, I think. Choir especially… there was something nice about everybody’s voices, and quilting kept my hands busy. But now when I think of them, it’s more like I know I ought to want to go, but I don’t feel that push.\n\nMost days I just end up at home, TV on low. Sometimes I know folks are expecting me, or I think maybe I should visit, and then I just… don’t. It’s not that I hate those things. The interest just doesn’t seem to carry me there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, even if not all the time, you feel down, sad, or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. Some days I feel kind of… flat, I guess, but I don’t always know if that’s sad. My daughter asks me that too sometimes, and then I start thinking about whether I slept right or if I ate, and then I forget what I was answering.\n\nSo, most days? I can’t say for sure. I haven’t noticed it like that, like crying or saying “I’m sad” every day. It’s more just sitting there and the day goes by.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a persistent sadness or low mood, even if it's not severe?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it persistent sadness. That sounds more… steady, like a rain that doesn’t stop. Mine isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m just quiet, or I’m sitting and the TV’s talking more than I am.\n\nLow, maybe some days. But most days? I get mixed up trying to say. Yesterday I meant to fold towels and then I found an old church bulletin in the drawer, and I was thinking about who used to sit in the third pew… so I don’t always keep track of my mood like that. I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think how to say it right. I don’t wake up and say, “I’m sad,” not most days like that. Sometimes I’m just sort of there, you know, and the house is quiet except the television.\n\nBut then I’ll start thinking about whether I fed the cat—well, I don’t have a cat now, but we had one years ago—and then my mind goes off. So I don’t know if that counts as low mood. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I know you asked me that already, and I’m trying not to go in circles. I just… I don’t really have a clear answer. Some days feel kind of dull, like the morning just turns into afternoon, but I don’t know if that means sad.\n\nI used to know the days by when the mailman came, because he’d come right after that game show—well, the old one, not the new host—and now I don’t pay attention the same. So, most days low? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it that plain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel a low or sad mood, even if it’s not very strong or overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m not giving you a straight yes or no. I just don’t really know. Some days I feel kind of flat, or quiet, but I don’t sit there thinking, “this is sadness.”\n\nIt’s like when you leave tea too long and it’s not bad, just not right anymore… my mother used to do that, leave the cup by the window. Anyway, I haven’t noticed it being most days in a way I could say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
